Wolbachia Mosquitoes Cut Dengue and Chikungunya Infections About 69% in Randomized Blood-Test Trial - Tech Times

News Brief
A cluster-randomized trial in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, found that releasing Wolbachia-infected Aedes aegypti mosquitoes reduced dengue and chikungunya infections by about 69%. The study, conducted over four years and involving 5,757 children aged 6 to 11, used blood tests to count infections rather than clinic visits, ensuring that cases not resulting in illness were also included. The trial was part of the EVITA Dengue initiative, which tested the effectiveness of Wolbachia in combination with standard spraying measures. However, the results are preliminary and not yet peer-reviewed, with no confidence intervals provided. The study's findings are limited to one city, and the effectiveness of Wolbachia may depend on local factors such as climate and mosquito ecology. The trial's design, which randomized entire neighborhoods rather than individual children, is noted as a key aspect of the methodology. While the results are promising, they do not provide definitive evidence of Wolbachia's impact on other viruses or populations outside the study area.
Original source text (English)
Tech Science Business Tech Health Culture Deals & Reviews Sign In Account Manage Account Logout Home Health Public Health Wolbachia Mosquitoes Cut Dengue and Chikungunya Infections About 69% in Randomized Blood-Test Trial Belo Horizonte trial counted infections by blood test, not clinic visits, with standard spraying kept in place By Morris Robinson Published: Nov 21 2026, 4:05 PM EST Share on Facebook Share on Twitter Share on LinkedIn Share on Reddit Share on Flipboard Share on WhatsApp Todd Montgomery, owner of Bee Safe Mosquito Control, disperses Aedes Albopictus mosquitos in Silver Spring, Maryland, on August 4, 2026. Todd Montgomery pops the lid off a small tube and taps out hundreds of mosquitoes into the backyard of a suburban home outside Washington. He's not up to mischief: these are males that don't bite, and they carry a gut bacteria called Wolbachia, which means when they mate with uninfected females, the eggs won't hatch. It is the first initiative of its kind aimed at residential customers in the United States, with 600,000 tiger mosquitoes (Aedes albopictus) set to be released across the region by the end of summer. Alex WROBLEWSKI/AFP via Getty Images A cluster-randomized trial in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, found about 69% fewer dengue and chikungunya infections in neighborhoods that received Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes, according to an Associated Press report on Nov. 21. The EVITA Dengue trial results were presented at the annual meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene (ASTMH). Researchers tested schoolchildren's blood for antibodies rather than counting diagnosed cases, and every neighborhood received the same pesticide and control measures, so the result shows what Wolbachia added on top of existing control. The timing matters for U.S. readers. Florida's weekly arbovirus report counted 651 locally acquired dengue cases, with 487 of them in Hillsborough County, which includes Tampa. Why Blood Tests Make 69% Hard To Compare With Earlier Results Counts based on diagnosed cases miss infections that never make anyone sick; the CDC says roughly three in four dengue infections cause no illness. EVITA Dengue instead tested children's blood for evidence of infection, a primary outcome its protocol calls sero-incidence of dengue, Zika and chikungunya, so it can count infections that never reached a clinic. That design is why the 69% figure cannot be lined up directly against earlier results. A randomized trial in Yogyakarta, Indonesia, reported a 77% reduction in dengue incidence and 86% fewer hospitalizations, counting laboratory-confirmed dengue among people who had fever. A study of city-wide releases in Niterói, Brazil, reported 89% lower notified dengue incidence compared with the decade before releases and estimated that the program averted roughly three-quarters of the dengue burden expected in the 2024 surge. The Niterói result is observational, not randomized, comparing time periods rather than randomized neighborhoods, and it counts notified cases rather than infections. Taken together, the three studies measure different outcomes with different designs. EVITA Dengue's lower headline number counts antibody-detected infections for two viruses at once. How Did EVITA Dengue Randomize Neighborhoods? The trial ran for four years and enrolled 5,757 children aged 6 to 11, with areas randomly assigned in two groups of 29 so that half received Wolbachia-carrying Aedes aegypti and the rest served as comparison areas. The published protocol defined 58 school catchment clusters randomized one to one. Randomizing whole areas rather than individual children suits a mosquito release, which cannot be aimed at selected individuals. The tradeoff is that cluster trials need more participants to reach the same statistical power as trials that randomize individuals. Brazilian outlet Bori reported a 68.6% overall reduction, with 68.5% for dengue and 73.3% for chikungunya. Bori's report did not mention confidence intervals, and those per-virus figures come only from that report. How Wolbachia Blocks Dengue Inside Aedes aegypti Mosquitoes Wolbachia is a common bacterium in many insects, but Aedes aegypti, the species that spreads dengue and chikungunya, does not carry it naturally. Once scientists introduce it, the bacterium reduces the mosquito's ability to transmit certain viruses; the World Mosquito Program, a nonprofit that pioneered the approach, describes mechanisms that include boosting the insect's immune response and competing with the virus for cholesterol. The program also says blocking is incomplete, so released mosquitoes can still carry some virus. The bacterium passes from mother to offspring, so once it is established it persists in the population. Researchers call this population replacement, which relies on releases of infected males and females and can become self-maintaining even after releases cease. The trial's goal was for 60% of trapped mosquitoes to carry Wolbachia, with remedial releases if a cluster fell short. One engineering constraint is temperature. The World Mosquito Program says high temperatures reduce Wolbachia density in mosquitoes and reduce maternal transmission, and a University of Melbourne explainer likewise notes that bacterial density can decrease under very hot conditions. Related Wolbachia-Infected Aedes Aegypti Mosquitoes to be Unleased in Singapore to Quash Dengue Threat Honduras Innovates in Dengue Epidemic Fight by Deploying Special Mosquitoes Why Self-Spreading Releases Differ From Male-Only Releases in Singapore and Google's Debug Replacement programs release both sexes so the bacterium becomes established in the local population, which is the World Mosquito Program's self-sustaining method used in Brazil and Indonesia. Other programs use suppression, releasing only males that carry Wolbachia, which can sterilize wild females they mate with; that shrinks the population but needs sustained effort, as in one study where males were released twice weekly for 24 weeks. In the United States, the Environmental Protection Agency regulates Wolbachia-carrying males as a pesticide and registered WB1 Males in April 2024, a product it describes as a population suppression tool. Google's Debug program applied for an EPA experimental use permit covering up to 16 million male mosquitoes per state in each of two years in California and Florida, and EPA accepted public comments through June 5, 2026. The notice names Culex quinquefasciatus males, not Aedes aegypti, the species that spreads dengue. Hamer Calls Results "Really Exciting" but Projection Remains Unproven Dr. David Hamer of Boston University, who was not involved in the trial, called the results "really exciting." He predicted that wider use of these mosquitoes, combined with existing control steps, could drive dengue to much lower levels in many places. That is a forecast, not a measured result. Dr. Sri Edupuganti of Emory University was part of the international research team. Conference Results, One City and No Zika Answer Limit What Trial Shows Four limits apply. The results are preliminary and not yet peer reviewed, having been presented at a conference rather than published in a journal, and Bori's report gave no confidence intervals. The trial covers one city, and Belo Horizonte's climate, housing and mosquito ecology may differ from other places. Too few Zika cases occurred to evaluate the effect on that virus. And the roughly 69% figure is a combined dengue and chikungunya result as AP reported it; per-virus figures appear only in Bori's report. 651 Local Dengue Cases in Florida Raise Release Strategy Questions for U.S. Officials AP reported that Florida's outbreak appears biggest in any U.S. state since at least the 1940s, and for decades most cases were in travelers infected abroad. The state's count of 651 locally acquired cases, about 75% of them in Hillsborough County, comes from its weekly arbovirus report. Hillsborough County aerially treated for adult mosquitoes from Oct. 27 to 30, citing an unusually high number of dengue cases. Officials weighing releases face questions of cost and scale. A randomized result showing a large drop in infections on top of existing control gives them a firmer evidence base than before-and-after comparisons alone. Readers who want to ask their local mosquito-control district a useful question can ask what it measures, infections or diagnosed cases, and whether its approach spreads on its own or needs repeated releases. Read more: American Samoa Dengue Outbreak: CDC Credits Faster Lab Testing but Cannot Show Fewer Cases Frequently Asked Questions Do Wolbachia mosquitoes prevent dengue? In this randomized trial, neighborhoods with released mosquitoes had about 69% fewer dengue and chikungunya infections than comparison neighborhoods, measured by blood tests, according to the AP. The results were presented at a conference, and Bori reported they are not yet peer reviewed or published in a journal. Why does measuring infections by blood test change how to read the result? Antibody tests can detect infections that never made anyone sick, while case counts cannot, so the figure is not directly comparable with the 77% reduction in Yogyakarta or the 89% notified-incidence drop in Niterói. What is the difference between replacement and suppression releases? Replacement releases both males and females so the bacterium spreads and persists in the local population, while suppression releases only males repeatedly to reduce the population. The EPA registered a suppression product called WB1 Males in April 2024. Does the trial say anything about Zika? No. Too few Zika cases occurred during the trial for researchers to evaluate an effect on that virus. c 2026 TECHTIMES.com All rights reserved. Do not reproduce without permission. 全球范围综述揭示气候变暖加速基孔肯雅热传播及地理扩散 - Bioengineer.org

Global Scoping Review Reveals Climate Warming Accelerates Chikungunya Transmission and Geographic Expansion - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报Bioengineer.org于2026年11月20日发布的一项全球范围综述显示,气候变暖正在加速基孔肯雅热的传播及地理扩散。由王超、李新耀及其同事领导的研究分析了2000年至2025年间发表的104项符合条件的研究,发现温度和降雨量显著影响该病毒的传播。历史上局限于热带地区的基孔肯雅热现已在超过100个国家和地区被确认,其中2025年8月底中国广东省佛山市报告了一次重大暴发,病例数超过10,000例。研究指出,虽然适中温度(23–30°C)有助于传播,但极端条件可能抑制其传播。综述还指出,对湿度和风等非热变量的实证评估仍有限,低收入和历史上流行地区面临更高的风险。作者提出了一种三级适应框架,以指导政策响应,强调需要提高全球卫生应急准备能力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2026年11月20日发表。研究评估了2000年1月1日至2025年11月1日之间发表的研究。2025年7月记录到病例显著激增,至2025年8月底,中国广东省佛山市报告了超过10,000例确诊病例。
地点与对象
基孔肯雅病毒已在全球范围内扩散,其本地传播已在超过100个国家和地区被确认。研究特别指出其传播至东南亚、南亚、印度洋岛屿以及美洲地区,其中中国广东省佛山市出现了一次显著的暴发。
事件
一项全球范围综述发现,气候变暖加速了基孔肯雅热的传播及地理扩散。由王超、李新耀及其同事领导的研究综合了104项符合条件的研究,确定气候因素是该病毒传播的关键驱动因素。
规模、影响与风险
该病毒已显示出显著的地理扩散趋势,在多个地区引发了重大疫情。有记录的最严重暴发发生在2025年中国广东省佛山市,确诊病例超过10,000例。综述指出,低收入和历史上流行地区仍面临日益加剧的疾病风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究采用系统框架进行,遵循PRISMA指南并进行了多数据库检索。研究确定了气候对基孔肯雅热传播的实证影响,并指出需要加强预防性准备。研究结果基于现有文献和预测模型的综合分析。

Global Scoping Review Reveals Climate Warming Accelerates Chikungunya Transmission and Geographic Expansion - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A global scoping review published on November 20, 2026, by Bioengineer.org reveals that climate warming is accelerating the transmission and geographic expansion of the chikungunya virus. The study, led by Chao Wang, Xinyao Li, and colleagues, analyzed 104 eligible studies published between 2000 and 2025, finding that temperature and rainfall significantly influence the virus's spread. Historically confined to tropical regions, chikungunya has now been confirmed in over 100 countries and territories, with a major outbreak in Foshan, Guangdong, China, reporting over 10,000 cases by August 2025. The research highlights that while moderate temperatures (23–30°C) enhance transmission, extreme conditions can suppress it. The review also notes that empirical evaluations of non-thermal variables like humidity and wind are limited, and that low-income and historically endemic regions face heightened risks. The authors propose a three-tier adaptation framework to guide policy responses, emphasizing the need for improved global health preparedness.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Tuesday, November 24, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Global Scoping Review Reveals Climate Warming Accelerates Chikungunya Transmission and Geographic Expansion by November 20, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Global Scoping Review Reveals Climate Warming Accelerates Chikungunya Transmission and Geographic Expansion Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A comprehensive scoping review evaluating a quarter-century of international research indicates that global climate change is actively altering the transmission intensity and geographical boundaries of the chikungunya virus. Led by Chao Wang, Xinyao Li, and colleagues, the systematic evaluation synthesized empirical climatic drivers, predictive ecological niche models, and public health adaptation strategies from 104 eligible studies published between January 1, 2000, and November 1, 2025. The investigators determined that while temperature and rainfall heavily modulate the biological kinetics of vector mosquitoes and viral replication, international research and preventive preparedness remain structurally imbalanced, leaving low-income and historically endemic regions vulnerable to escalating disease risks. Historically confined to localized tropical reservoirs following its initial identification in Tanzania during the 1950s, chikungunya virus—an arbovirus of the genus Alphavirus primarily transmitted by Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus mosquitoes—has rapidly expanded across the globe. Driven by climatic variability and heightened human mobility, autochthonous transmission has now been confirmed in more than 100 countries and territories. The pathogen has demonstrated substantial geographic momentum, initiating major epidemics across Southeast Asia, South Asia, Indian Ocean islands, and the Americas, where a continuous wave of infection took hold after late 2013. A significant surge documented in July 2025 led to more than 10,000 confirmed cases in Foshan, Guangdong, China, by the end of August, marking the country’s largest outbreak on record and underscoring how changing weather parameters amplify transmission in subtropical areas. The methodological framework of the review adhered strictly to the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic reviews and Meta-Analyses Extension for Scoping Reviews guidelines, following an a priori protocol registered on the Open Science Framework. The researchers performed multi-database searches across PubMed, Web of Science, Scopus, and EBSCOhost, screening 2,336 initial records down to 104 qualifying English-language studies based on the Population, Concept, and Context framework. Methodological quality was evaluated using the Joanna Briggs Institute Critical Appraisal Checklist, and the identified literature was categorized into empirical observations quantifying direct climatic impacts, future mathematical projections, and adaptation measures organized in alignment with the World Health Organization Global Arbovirus Initiative framework. Discover more Check Symptoms Study Biology Track Weather Check Forecasts Find Treatments A critical temporal analysis of the literature demonstrated an abrupt surge in research output over the last decade. Between 2000 and 2014, global scientific databases cataloged a mere seven qualifying studies examining climate-related chikungunya dynamics. From 2015 onward, publication numbers expanded dramatically to 97 articles, spurred in substantial part by the release of milestone assessments including the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change Fifth Assessment Report in 2014 and the Lancet Commission on Health and Climate Change in 2015. These international scientific syntheses catalyzed widespread efforts to measure the explicit epidemiological outcomes of atmospheric warming on arboviral spread, while shifting outbreak clusters in temperate Asian regions during 2025 subsequently spurred local empirical research interest. Synthesizing the empirical evidence across 48 climate-association studies, the researchers identified ambient temperature as the fundamental environmental driver of viral dissemination. Among 38 studies analyzing ambient thermal parameters, 36 reported a positive correlation with transmission metrics, while two documented suppressive effects under extreme conditions. Through a narrative synthesis of these investigations, the authors established a consolidated optimal transmission window between 23 degrees Celsius and 30 degrees Celsius. Within this specific thermal band, elevated temperatures accelerate viral replication, significantly shorten the extrinsic incubation period of the virus within mosquito hosts, enhance vector population growth, and increase adult survival rates. Conversely, temperatures falling below 17 degrees Celsius markedly reduce vector competence, whereas extreme heat exceeding 35 degrees Celsius diminishes mosquito survival and impairs the catalytic activity of viral ribonucleic acid polymerase. Discover more health Get Health Tips Health Rainfall and hydrological variations demonstrated a complex, dual effect across 32 analyzed studies, with 15 reporting positive correlations, 15 documenting negative associations, and two identifying geographically divergent outcomes. Moderate precipitation establishes standing water reserves essential for mosquito reproduction and larval development, with soil moisture anomalies directly enhancing transmission potential in tropical semi-arid zones. However, excessive precipitation and heavy storms produce a flushing effect that mechanically washes out established aquatic habitats, an inhibitory phenomenon particularly pronounced in high-altitude montane environments. Similarly, severe moisture deficits suppress dissemination by desiccating oviposition sites. Despite these opposing local mechanisms, the synthesis revealed a consistent biological time lag of one to four weeks between major precipitation events and corresponding spikes in clinical human cases. Atmospheric humidity was investigated in 11 studies, where moderate to elevated relative humidity was largely shown to extend larval survival, prolong breeding site stability, and increase egg production, as observed in field investigations across Iran and Spain. Conversely, persistently high relative humidity exceeding 70 percent demonstrated negative correlations with vector propagation in certain mid-to-high latitude settings, such as southern Brazil, illustrating that environmental suppression occurs when moisture conditions exceed the physiological adaptation range of local mosquito species. However, the review noted that empirical evaluations of non-thermal variables remain scarce, with factors like wind direction, wind speed, and microclimatic ground moisture largely neglected in published literature despite their clear influence on passive vector dispersal and larval habitat persistence. Beyond historical associations, six empirical investigations provided documented evidence that ongoing climate change has already altered local disease ecology. Longitudinal field data from Ecuador spanning 1988 to 2024 revealed that mosquito habitats advanced northward by 45 kilometers per decade, as warmer winters lowered egg mortality and yielded a 2.5-fold increase in locally acquired cases in temperate highland areas. In Brazil’s tropical savanna, concurrent heatwaves and altered rainfall regimes boosted transmission intensity by 30 percent compared to stable baseline periods. Similarly, prior to the large 2025 outbreak in Foshan, China, the region experienced 38 cumulative days of suitable mosquito propagation conditions, including an unbroken 23-day peak window that directly facilitated widespread community transmission. Evaluating 50 forward-looking modeling studies, the review established that ecological niche models across all Shared Socioeconomic Pathways—ranging from sustainability-oriented SSP1-2.6 to fossil-fueled SSP5-8.5 scenarios—predict substantial spatial expansions of chikungunya risk by 2040, 2060, 2080, and 2100. These predictive projections consistently signal a transboundary expansion toward higher latitudes and higher altitudes, exposing immunologically naive populations in temperate zones. Predictive models indicate novel transmission risks emerging in Germany and Italy as warmer temperatures lengthen vector activity windows, while modeling for Australia indicates escalating epidemic potential by 2029 along eastern and northern coastal belts. Projections for North America indicate that climatic suitability for local transmission will extend into Canada by 2040 and 2070 under both Representative Concentration Pathway 4.5 and 8.5 scenarios. To guide policy responses, the authors formulated an integrated three-tier adaptation framework synthesizing 65 studies that addressed intervention strategies. The bottom tier focuses on foundational prevention and structural resilience, accounting for 22 percent of the adaptation literature; it encompasses urban water infrastructure improvements, habitat elimination through spatial planning, community engagement, and targeted vaccine development. The mid-tier represents predictive surveillance and early warning systems, which dominate 60 percent of documented approaches through climate-based forecasting and integrated entomological-clinical tracking. The top tier encompasses reactive emergency management, comprising 18 percent of studies, which addresses acute outbreak suppression, clinical workforce preparedness, and chemical vector control scheduled around climatic activity peaks to prevent insecticide resistance evolution. Crucially, the review identified severe geographical and economic disparities that impede equitable global health defense. Empirical studies have clustered disproportionately in Europe and South America following recent high-profile outbreaks, whereas the historical epicenter of the virus in Africa and vulnerable low-income territories remain profoundly underrepresented. When classified by World Bank income brackets, the overwhelming majority of adaptation literature originates from high-income and upper-middle-income nations focusing on sophisticated mid-tier warning systems. This leaves resource-constrained, lower-income populations without validated evidence for foundational infrastructural adaptation, underscoring an urgent imperative to direct international funding toward decentralized environmental management, empirical field trials, and cross-border One Health collaborations. View five news elements
Time
The study was published on November 20, 2026. The research evaluated studies published between January 1, 2000, and November 1, 2025. A significant surge in cases was documented in July 2025, with over 10,000 confirmed cases in Foshan, Guangdong, China, by the end of August 2025.
Location and population
The chikungunya virus has expanded globally, with confirmed autochthonous transmission in more than 100 countries and territories. The study specifically highlights its spread to Southeast Asia, South Asia, Indian Ocean islands, and the Americas, with a notable outbreak in Foshan, Guangdong, China.
Event
A global scoping review found that climate warming accelerates chikungunya transmission and geographic expansion. The study, led by Chao Wang, Xinyao Li, and colleagues, synthesized 104 eligible studies and identified climate factors as key drivers of the virus's spread.
Scale, impact and risk
The virus has demonstrated substantial geographic momentum, initiating major epidemics in multiple regions. The largest outbreak on record occurred in Foshan, Guangdong, China, with over 10,000 confirmed cases. The review highlights that low-income and historically endemic regions remain vulnerable to escalating disease risks.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was conducted using a systematic framework, adhering to PRISMA guidelines and multi-database searches. It identified empirical evidence of climate impacts on chikungunya transmission and noted the need for improved preventive preparedness. The findings are based on a synthesis of existing literature and predictive models.

巴西研究发现携带沃尔巴克氏菌的改良蚊虫可使登革热和基孔肯雅热感染减少69% - 《印度时报》

Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes - The Times of India
新闻简报一项在巴西伯利恒市开展的研究发现,释放携带沃尔巴克氏菌的改良蚊虫显著降低了超过5,700名儿童中的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率,降幅约为69%。这项研究于2026年11月21日在美国热带医学与卫生学会会议上发表,比较了释放改良蚊虫的社区与未释放社区之间的感染率。该策略的有效性在巴西2024年严重的登革热暴发期间也得到了证实。然而,由于缺乏病例,该研究无法可靠地评估其对寨卡病毒感染的影响。世界蚊虫项目一直在全球推广这种方法,此前在印度尼西亚的试验也显示出类似的结果。研究结果表明,将携带沃尔巴克氏菌的蚊虫与现有的蚊虫控制措施结合,可能有助于减少许多地区的登革热和基孔肯雅热传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究历时四年,研究结果于2026年11月21日公布。巴西严重的登革热暴发发生在2024年。
地点与对象
该研究在巴西伯利恒市进行,涉及超过5,700名儿童。研究结果还提到了佛罗里达州的登革热暴发,但这不是主要研究地点。
事件
一项巴西研究发现,在释放携带沃尔巴克氏菌的改良蚊虫的地区,儿童的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率减少了69%。该研究在热带医学与卫生学会的会议上进行了展示。
规模、影响与风险
该研究报告称,目标地区登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率几乎下降了69%。它还指出,这种保护效果在巴西2024年严重的登革热暴发期间依然有效。然而,由于病例不足,该研究无法可靠地得出对寨卡病毒感染影响的结论。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究涉及释放携带沃尔巴克氏菌的蚊虫,并比较了释放和未释放社区之间的感染率。世界蚊虫项目一直在推动扩大这种做法。研究结果基于血液检测,被视为强有力的证据,尽管该研究并未专门针对基孔肯雅热。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes - The Times of India

News Brief
A study conducted in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, found that releasing modified mosquitoes carrying Wolbachia bacteria significantly reduced dengue and chikungunya infections by about 69% among over 5,700 children. The research, presented at the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene meeting on November 21, 2026, involved comparing infection rates between neighborhoods with and without the mosquito release. The effectiveness of the strategy was noted even during Brazil's severe dengue outbreak in 2024. However, the study could not reliably assess its impact on Zika infections due to a lack of cases. The World Mosquito Program has been promoting this approach globally, with earlier trials in Indonesia showing similar results. The findings suggest that combining Wolbachia mosquitoes with existing mosquito-control measures could help reduce dengue and chikungunya transmission in many regions.

Edition IN IN US UK GCC English English हिन्दी मराठी ಕನ್ನಡ தமிழ் বাংলা മലയാളം తెలుగు ગુજરાતી delhi24.2°C Subscribe to TOI+ Sign In TOI Science Nature Archaeology Wildlife Earth Space Discovery Today's ePaper Live Trending Patrick And Brittany Mahomes Delhi Encounter Brent Underwood Alabama Woman News Nayana Motamma Resignation News Science News Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes Edited by Vijaya Srivastava / TIMESOFINDIA.COM / Nov 21, 2026, 21:38 IST Save Comments Share Aa +Text Size Small Medium Large Print File photo of a mosquito feeling on hand (AP Photo/Rick Bowmer, File) Scientists have reported strong new evidence that specially bred mosquitoes carrying a naturally occurring bacteria can significantly reduce the spread of dengue and chikungunya, after a major study involving more than 5,700 children in Brazil found a nearly 69 per cent drop in infections in areas where the mosquitoes were released.The four-year study, conducted in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, found that children living in neighbourhoods with these mosquitoes were far less likely to contract the two mosquito-borne viruses than those in similar areas without them. The protection remained effective even during Brazil's severe dengue outbreak in 2024, researchers said on Saturday.The findings were presented at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene. They offer fresh support for using Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes as an alternative to relying only on pesticides and measures to prevent mosquito bites.“This is really exciting,” said Dr David Hamer, a global health expert at Boston University who was not involved in the study.He said combining the release of Wolbachia mosquitoes with existing mosquito-control measures could “knock dengue down to much lower levels” in many places. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like Dermatologists Beg Women With Thin Hair To Use This Household ItemTop Austin Hairstylist Learn More Undo The findings come as Florida faces an unusually large dengue outbreak, which appears to be the biggest recorded in any US state since at least the 1940s.Dengue cases in the continental US are usually linked to international travel. However, Florida health officials had recorded more than 650 locally acquired infections as of Friday. About three-quarters of these cases were reported in Hillsborough County, which includes Tampa. How the mosquito strategy works The approach involves breeding mosquitoes with Wolbachia, a type of bacteria that occurs naturally in many insects. The bacteria are harmless to humans but are not commonly found in Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, which spread dengue.Wolbachia significantly reduces the mosquitoes' ability to transmit certain viruses. Female mosquitoes also pass the bacteria on to their offspring.Scientists release both male and female mosquitoes carrying the bacteria into a targeted area. As they breed with the local mosquito population, Wolbachia gradually becomes established. This reduces the ability of the wider mosquito population to spread viruses.The nonprofit World Mosquito Program has been leading efforts to develop and expand this approach outside the US. Earlier field trials in Indonesia and other locations have also shown significant reductions in dengue cases. What the Brazil study found The latest research went beyond tracking reported infections. Researchers tested children's blood for evidence of dengue, chikungunya and Zika infections. This helped identify infections that may have gone undiagnosed because people experienced only mild symptoms.The study followed more than 5,700 children over four years. All participating neighbourhoods received the same pesticide treatments and other mosquito-control measures. They then compared infection rates between children living in the two groups of neighbourhoods.The results showed about a 69 per cent reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections among children in areas where the specially bred mosquitoes were released.Researchers could not establish whether the strategy reduced Zika infections because there were too few cases to draw a reliable conclusion. 细菌驱动的蚊子将登革热和基孔肯雅热病例减少69% | 每日Sabah - 每日Sabah

Bacteria-powered mosquitoes cut dengue, chikungunya cases by 69% | Daily Sabah - Daily Sabah
新闻简报一项在巴西伯利恒市进行的研究,涉及超过5700名儿童,发现释放携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子到社区中,与采用标准蚊虫控制干预措施的地区相比,登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率减少了69%。这种效果在巴西2024年严重登革热暴发期间依然持续。这项研究在美洲热带医学与卫生学会会议上报告,表明这些携带限制病毒传播细菌的蚊子可能是一种有效对抗登革热和基孔肯雅热的策略。尽管该研究未报告足够多的寨卡病毒病例以评估其对这种病毒的影响,但像大卫·哈默博士这样的专家预测,将沃尔巴克氏体蚊子与现有措施结合,可能在许多地区显著降低登革热水平。这些发现突显了一种有前景的替代方案,以传统基于农药的蚊虫控制方法。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究历时四年,结果于2026年11月22日报告。病例减少69%的现象出现在巴西2024年严重的登革热暴发期间。
地点与对象
该研究在巴西伯利恒市进行,涉及超过5700名儿童,这些儿童居住在有和没有携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子的社区中。
事件
研究人员报告称,在释放携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子的社区中,学童的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率比采用标准蚊虫控制干预措施的类似社区减少了69%。
规模、影响与风险
该研究发现登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率显著下降,携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子区域病例减少了约69%。这种效果在巴西2024年严重的登革热暴发期间依然持续。然而,该研究未报告足够多的寨卡病毒病例,以确定沃尔巴克氏体蚊子对该病毒的影响。
应对、状态与不确定性
这些发现是在美洲热带医学与卫生学会的会议上报告的。研究通过检测血液中的登革热、基孔肯雅热或寨卡病毒迹象来进行,结果由研究人员确认。该策略正在考虑更广泛的应用,专家预测它可能显著降低登革热水平。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bacteria-powered mosquitoes cut dengue, chikungunya cases by 69% | Daily Sabah - Daily Sabah

News Brief
A study conducted in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, involving over 5,700 children found that releasing Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes into neighborhoods led to a 69% reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections compared to areas with standard mosquito-control interventions. This effect was sustained even during Brazil’s severe 2024 dengue outbreak. The research, presented at the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene meeting, suggests that these mosquitoes, which carry a bacteria that limits virus transmission, could be a viable strategy to combat dengue and chikungunya. While the study did not report enough Zika cases to assess the impact on that virus, experts like Dr. David Hamer predict that combining Wolbachia mosquitoes with existing measures could significantly lower dengue levels in many regions. The findings highlight a promising alternative to traditional pesticide-based approaches in mosquito control.
Original source text (English)
Bacteria-powered mosquitoes cut dengue, chikungunya cases by 69% A mosquito feeds on a technician at the Salt Lake City Mosquito Abatement District, Salt Lake City, Utah, U.S., July 26, 2023. (AP Photo) by Associated Press Nov 22, 2026 10:50 am Children who lived in Brazilian neighborhoods buzzing with a special kind of mosquito were far less likely to catch dengue or another worrisome virus, chikungunya , researchers reported Saturday. The major study of more than 5,700 children in one of the world’s dengue hot spots offers new evidence that breeding mosquitoes unable to transmit these viruses can be a viable strategy rather than solely relying on pesticides and trying to avoid getting bitten. In neighborhoods populated by these so-called Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes, researchers found about a 69% reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections in schoolchildren compared to similar neighborhoods - an effect sustained even during Brazil’s severe 2024 dengue outbreak, said Dr. Sri Edupuganti of Emory University, part of the international research team. The researchers reported the findings at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene. "This is really exciting,” said Dr. David Hamer, a global health expert at Boston University who wasn’t involved with the Brazilian study. He predicts that pairing more use of these Wolbachia mosquitoes with existing steps to fight mosquito-borne disease could "knock dengue down to much lower levels” in many places. The latest research comes as Florida grapples with an unusually large outbreak of the virus, what appears to be the biggest in any U.S. state since at least the 1940s . Usually in the continental U.S., dengue is found mostly in people who traveled internationally. But as of Friday, Florida health officials had tallied more than 650 locally acquired cases, about three-quarters of them in Hillsborough County, home to Tampa. Outside the U.S., the nonprofit World Mosquito Program has pioneered the Wolbachia strategy. How it works: Scientists breed mosquitoes to carry a kind of bacteria called Wolbachia. It is harmless to people and common in many insects, although not dengue-carrying Aedes aegypti mosquitoes. The bacteria dramatically reduce the mosquitoes’ ability to transmit certain viruses. Female mosquitoes spread the bacteria to their offspring, so releasing enough of the specially bred insects - males and females - into an area allows Wolbachia to gradually become established in the local mosquito population. Field trials and other studies in Indonesia and elsewhere have shown the strategy can significantly lower dengue diagnoses. But because people with mild symptoms may not be diagnosed, the new study in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, went further. Researchers tested blood for signs of dengue, chikungunya or the Zika virus. The four-year study compared children living in neighborhoods that all got the same pesticide and other mosquito-control interventions, but half were randomly assigned to have Wolbachia mosquitoes released into the area. There were too few cases of Zika to calculate if the special mosquitoes had any effect on that disease. Last Update: November 22, 2026 13:13 Mosquitoes virus health
View five news elements
Time
The study was conducted over four years, with results reported on November 22, 2026. The 69% reduction in cases was observed during Brazil’s severe 2024 dengue outbreak.
Location and population
The study took place in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, involving over 5,700 children in neighborhoods with and without Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes.
Event
Researchers reported a 69% reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections among schoolchildren in neighborhoods where Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes were released, compared to similar neighborhoods with standard mosquito-control interventions.
Scale, impact and risk
The study found a significant reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections, with about 69% fewer cases in areas with Wolbachia mosquitoes. The effect was sustained during a severe dengue outbreak in Brazil in 2024. However, the study did not report enough Zika cases to determine the impact of Wolbachia mosquitoes on that virus.
Response, status and uncertainty
The findings were reported at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene. The study involved testing blood for signs of dengue, chikungunya, or Zika, and the results were confirmed by researchers. The strategy is being considered for broader application, with experts predicting it could reduce dengue levels significantly.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

与登革热、基孔肯雅热感染下降69%相关的特殊蚊子 - 《海湾新闻》

Special mosquitoes linked to 69% drop in dengue, chikungunya infections - Gulf News
新闻简报一项于2026年11月21日发表在《海湾新闻》上的研究指出,在巴西伯利奥尼市(Belo Horizonte)超过5700名儿童所在的地区,释放携带沃尔巴克氏体(Wolbachia)的蚊子后,登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率下降了69%。这项研究在美洲热带医学与卫生学会(American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene)会议上展示,表明这些特殊蚊子可能成为对抗蚊媒病毒的有效策略,减少对农药的依赖。研究发现,释放携带沃尔巴克氏体蚊子的社区与未释放的类似社区相比,感染病例显著减少,即使在巴西2024年严重的登革热暴发期间也是如此。然而,由于数据不足,无法确定该策略对寨卡病毒病例的影响。世界蚊子计划(World Mosquito Program)正在全球范围内推进这一策略,印尼的实地试验显示了积极的结果。这一进展为疾病控制工作带来了新的希望,特别是在登革热和基孔肯雅热高发地区。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年11月21日(发表日期),研究结果报告时间为2026年11月20日(事件日期)
地点与对象
巴西伯利奥尼市,涉及超过5700名儿童所在的社区,这些社区释放了携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子。
事件
一项研究发现,在释放携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子的社区中,学校儿童的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率比未释放的类似社区下降了69%。研究结果由美洲热带医学与卫生学会会议的研究人员报告。
规模、影响与风险
该研究观察到目标社区中登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率显著下降了69%,但由于数据不足,无法确定对寨卡病毒病例的影响。研究建议,使用携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子可能是一种可行的策略,有助于降低许多地区的疾病水平。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是国际努力的一部分,研究结果在科学会议上展示。研究团队包括埃默里大学的Sri Edupuganti博士和波士顿大学的David Hamer博士。该策略已在印尼及其他地区进行测试,显示出减少登革热诊断的潜力。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Special mosquitoes linked to 69% drop in dengue, chikungunya infections - Gulf News

News Brief
A study published in Gulf News on November 21, 2026, highlights a 69% drop in dengue and chikungunya infections among over 5,700 children in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, where Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes were released. The research, presented at the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene meeting, suggests that these special mosquitoes could be a viable strategy to combat mosquito-borne viruses, reducing the need for reliance on pesticides. The study found that neighborhoods with Wolbachia mosquitoes had significantly fewer infections compared to similar areas without them, even during Brazil’s severe 2024 dengue outbreak. However, the impact on Zika virus cases could not be determined due to insufficient data. The World Mosquito Program has been working on this strategy globally, with field trials in Indonesia showing promising results. This development offers new hope for disease control efforts, particularly in regions heavily affected by dengue and chikungunya.

Facebook X Whatsapp E-mail Linkedin Researchers say bacteria-carrying mosquitoes could strengthen existing disease-control efforts. AP Washington: Children who lived in Brazilian neighborhoods buzzing with a special kind of mosquito were far less likely to catch dengue or another worrisome virus, chikungunya, researchers reported Saturday. The major study of more than 5,700 children in one of the world’s dengue hot spots offers new evidence that breeding mosquitoes unable to transmit these viruses can be a viable strategy rather than solely relying on pesticides and trying to avoid getting bitten. Get updated faster and for FREE: Download the Gulf News app now - simply click here. In neighborhoods populated by these so-called Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes, researchers found about a 69% reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections in schoolchildren compared to similar neighborhoods — an effect sustained even during Brazil’s severe 2024 dengue outbreak, said Dr. Sri Edupuganti of Emory University, part of the international research team. The researchers reported the findings at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene. “This is really exciting,” said Dr. David Hamer, a global health expert at Boston University who wasn’t involved with the Brazilian study. He predicts that pairing more use of these Wolbachia mosquitoes with existing steps to fight mosquito-borne disease could “knock dengue down to much lower levels” in many places. The latest research comes as Florida grapples with an unusually large outbreak of the virus, what appears to be the biggest in any U.S. state since at least the 1940s. Usually in the continental U.S., dengue is found mostly in people who traveled internationally. But as of Friday, Florida health officials had tallied more than 650 locally acquired cases, about three-quarters of them in Hillsborough County, home to Tampa. Are you a mosquito magnet? Scientists reveal why you get bitten more Outside the U.S., the nonprofit World Mosquito Program has pioneered the Wolbachia strategy. How special mosquitoes help fight dengue How it works: Scientists breed mosquitoes to carry a kind of bacteria called Wolbachia. It is harmless to people and common in many insects, although not dengue-carrying Aedes aegypti mosquitoes. The bacteria dramatically reduce the mosquitoes’ ability to transmit certain viruses. Female mosquitoes spread the bacteria to their offspring, so releasing enough of the specially bred insects — males and females — into an area allows Wolbachia to gradually become established in the local mosquito population. Field trials and other studies in Indonesia and elsewhere have shown the strategy can significantly lower dengue diagnoses. But because people with mild symptoms may not be diagnosed, the new study in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, went further. Researchers tested blood for signs of dengue, chikungunya or the Zika virus. The four-year study compared children living in neighborhoods that all got the same pesticide and other mosquito-control interventions, but half were randomly assigned to have Wolbachia mosquitoes released into the area. There were too few cases of Zika to calculate if the special mosquitoes had any effect on that disease. Event
A study found a 69% reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections among schoolchildren in neighborhoods with Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes, compared to similar neighborhoods without them. The findings were reported by researchers at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene.
Scale, impact and risk

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报文章报道了《The Innovation Life》上发表的一项关于2025 基孔肯雅病毒(CHIKV) 暴发在中国的抗原和基因组分析研究。该研究聚焦于CHIKV的东/中/南非系(ECSA),该病毒株在暴发期间被确认为主要流行株。文章指出,中国报告了超过3,000例病例,病毒显示出潜在的免疫逃逸能力。研究还探讨了特定HLA等位基因,如HLA-C*07:02和HLA-C*01:02,在疾病进展中的作用。研究结果基于GISAID和其他来源的数据,研究时间跨度为2025-01-18至2026-09-08。文章未明确提及风险等级,但分析提供了病毒行为及其对公共卫生应对措施的潜在影响的见解。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2025-01-18 to 2026-09-08.
地点与对象
中国,重点关注2025年基孔肯雅病毒(CHIKV)暴发及其抗原性和遗传性分析。
事件
在中国进行了2025年基孔肯雅病毒 暴发的抗原性和遗传性分析,并发表于《The Innovation Life》。研究结果涉及CHIKV的东/中/南非系(ECSA)。
规模、影响与风险
研究报道了2025年中国CHIKV感染病例超过3,000例,重点关注ECSA系。分析强调了病毒免疫逃逸的潜力以及特定HLA等位基因在疾病进展中的作用,但研究中未明确提及风险等级。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究发表于《The Innovation Life》,包含对暴发的详细分析。研究引用了多个数据来源,包括GISAID,并讨论了病毒基因组和抗原性特征对公共卫生应对措施的潜在影响。研究中未报告未解决的信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

Original title (Chinese)
新浪财经APP缩小字体放大字体收藏微博分享MD
News Brief
The article reports on an antigenic and genetic analysis of the 2025 Chikungunya virus (CHIKV) outbreak in China, published in The Innovation Life. The study focused on the East/Central/South African lineage (ECSA) of CHIKV, which was identified as the dominant strain during the outbreak. It noted that over 3,000 cases were reported in China, with the virus showing potential for immune evasion. The research also examined the role of specific HLA alleles, such as HLA-C*07:02 and HLA-C*01:02, in disease progression. The findings were based on data from GISAID and other sources, and the study was conducted between 2025-01-18 and 2026-09-08. No explicit risk level was stated, but the analysis provided insights into the virus's behavior and its implications for public health responses.

VIPè ̄3⁄4ç ̈æ ̈è APPä ̧äo«ç ́æ çé ̈æ ̈è æ¶èμ· 24å°æ¶æ»å ̈ææ¥ææ°çè ́¢ç»èμè® ̄åè§é¢ï1⁄4æ ́å¤ç2ä ̧ç¦å©æ«æäoç» ́ç å 3æ3 ̈ï1⁄4sinafinanceï1⁄4
View five news elements
Time
2025-01-18 to 2026-09-08.
Location and population
China, with a focus on the Chikungunya virus (CHIKV) outbreak and its antigenic and genetic analysis.
Event
An antigenic and genetic analysis of the 2025 Chikungunya virus outbreak in China was conducted and published in The Innovation Life, with findings related to the East/Central/South African lineage (ECSA) of CHIKV.
Scale, impact and risk
The study reported over 3,000 cases of CHIKV infection in China during 2025, with a focus on the ECSA lineage. The analysis highlighted the potential for immune evasion and the role of specific HLA alleles in disease progression, but no explicit risk level was stated in the evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was published in The Innovation Life and included detailed analysis of the outbreak. It referenced data from multiple sources, including GISAID, and discussed the implications of the genetic and antigenic characteristics of the virus. No unresolved information was reported in the evidence.

基孔肯雅热的11个关键问题,一次说清

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

基孔肯雅热是由基孔肯雅病毒引起,主要经伊蚊叮咬传播的病毒性传染病,临床表现主要为发热、皮疹、关节和肌肉疼痛,多数患者1周内会好转,但30%—40%患者关节疼痛可能会持续数月甚至数年,对生活质量造成显著长期影响。-1-基孔肯雅病毒如何传播基孔肯雅病毒主要通过携带病毒的伊蚊叮咬传播,最常见的是埃及伊蚊和白纹伊蚊。

基孔肯雅热的11个关键问题,一次说清

基孔肯雅热是由基孔肯雅病毒引起,主要经伊蚊叮咬传播的病毒性传染病,临床表现主要为发热、皮疹、关节和肌肉疼痛,多数患者1周内会好转,但30%—40%患者关节疼痛可能会持续数月甚至数年,对生活质量造成显著长期影响。-1-基孔肯雅病毒如何传播基孔肯雅病毒主要通过携带病毒的伊蚊叮咬传播,最常见的是埃及伊蚊和白纹伊蚊。
